  Found 9 web pages
  You have an hour, and the goal is clear: see the Plan 9 guest's frame (the
  floor, the eight agents, the 144 workers) from inside a WebXR session on your
  host, viewing from any angle.

  The pieces exist, but they don't connect directly. Plan 9's draw(2) library
  writes to a local screen or a rio window . WebXR runs in a browser, and its
  sessions are HTML/WebGL contexts . The bridge between them has to be a stream
  of pixels from the guest to the host browser, with the browser rendering
  that stream into an XR scene.

  Here is the plan for the hour, in three stages.

  Stage 1: Get the guest's frame out as a stream (15 minutes)
  The zframe server already holds the state (eight segments, the tick). What it
  doesn't do is render the floor. The floor.c program does render, using
  draw(2), but it draws to the guest's VGA console, which you can't see from
  the host.

  The fastest path is to have floor.c (or a new zstream.c) draw the floor to an
  in-memory image instead of the screen, then write that image's bytes to a
  file or a pipe that the host can rea
  ──── (377 lines hidden) ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  orld as a 9P filesystem /usr/space/users/<tag>/ on the CPU server
  XR view the Wirth template, lazily resolved     the bootable USB's own Plan 9
  XR transport    9P over TCP, from any client    Unix 8th Ed (SIMH), Plan 9
  QEMU, GCE, Raspberry Pi
  And the VGA path is the key: the USB token boots Plan 9 on your hardware, so
  the guest's draw(2) screen is the XR view, and the XR session renders it. The
  USB token is not a fallback — it's the first client.

  What the USB token enables
  The transcript's earlier plan was: guest (QEMU) → stream → host browser → XR.
  That had three hops.

  The USB token collapses it to two:

  text
  Plan 9 on the USB token (draw(2) → the floor)
      ↓ 9P
  any client (a browser on the LAN, or another Plan 9)
      ↓ WebXR
  the headset
  The USB token is the carrier — it draws the floor with draw(2), serves the
  frame over 9P, and any client can join. The XR client reads the 9P files and
  renders.

  The 9P structure
  text
  /usr/space/
    users/
      METATRON/
        pos     the (u, v, layer) as text: "0.5 0.3 3"
        rot     the quaternion as text: "0.0 0.38 0.0 0.92"
        mesh    the avatar SVG as bytes
        state   the 16-bit state, as 4 hex chars
      SOLOMON/
        ...
    world/
      floor     the plane's (u, v) and radius
      clock     the tick (0–59)
      frame     the 65,536-byte RGBA frame from floor.c
  Writes to pos move the agent. Reads block until the next change (Plan 9's
  read on a file blocks natively). That's the pub/sub.

  The Wirth lazy resolution
  The template is a .wsn file that describes the XR scene. zlazy resolves it
  lazily:

  text
  XRSCENE = CAMERA { ENTITY } .
  CAMERA  = "camera" " " POSITION " " ROTATION .
  ENTITY  = "entity" " " IDENTIFIER " " POSITION " " ROTATION " " SCALE .
  And zlazy reads /usr/space/users/<tag>/pos only when it reaches that entity.
  So the parser evaluates only what the viewport needs.

  The build path
  Stage   What    Where
  1       QEMU (9legacy) with the existing floor.c        your laptop
  2       QEMU with the 9P server (9pserve)       your laptop
  3       USB token on real hardware      the Supermicro or the Raspberry Pi
  4       the XR client (browser, A-Frame)        any LAN device
  5       the XR headset  the client
  And the build-usb script in 9legacy/build is the tool that writes the USB
  image. You already have the image at
  /home/main/Programs/boxed/_archive/_inbox/plan9-usb.img.bz2.

  What to do first
  The USB token boots and runs floor.c. That's the test: does the floor draw on
  the USB's VGA?

  Then the 9P server: does the frame come out over 9P?

  Then the XR client: does the browser see it?

● I'll verify the underlying assumptions first—checking if the boxed push
  completed, whether 9P is available on host and guest, inspecting the USB
  image, and seeing what space/ currently renders in WebXR—before drafting a
  plan.

      3<>/dev/tcp/127.0.0.1/10…)

  Because Plan 9 avoids the abstraction layers of modern containerization or
  monolithic web stacks, the low-power Supermicro X7SPA-H-D525 or X7SLA becomes
  an ideal, bulletproof node for this setup:
  • Network Performance: The dual Intel Gigabit NICs can be bound—one to listen
  to the incoming remote HTTP web traffic, and the other to sync raw 9P state
  updates with other back-end CPU/Auth nodes in your cluster.
  • Low Latency, Low Compute: A dual-core Atom processor would struggle to run
  modern heavy web apps. However, parsing simple Wirth strings and piping files
  from a 9P file tree takes negligible CPU power. You get near-instantaneous
  execution loops because the OS is effectively acting as the database, cache,
  and router all in one layer.

  That gives two different views, and you probably want both:
  1. The guest's own frame, seen from inside. This is the VGA image itself,
     shown as a textured plane in an XR scene. It's a flat picture, so moving
     round it shows a picture from different angles.
  2. The agents seen from any angle. For that the browser has to build the 3D
     itself, from live state that Plan 9 serves: each agent's hue, K state and
     tick. space/'s existing 3D scene can render it.

  Stages:
  1. Frame out (no changes to the guest). A host loop takes screendump frames
     through the monitor (console.py already does this) and writes them as PNGs.
     A small page shows them on a plane inside xr.html. This is the fastest way
     to see the guest's VGA in XR.
  2. State out (needs changes to the guest). A Plan 9 server built on httpd(2)
     serves the floor's states, read with mach(2) the way observe.c does, plus
     zframe's tick, as plain text. run.sh needs an extra port forward, which
     means restarting QEMU and setting up the graphics again. The scene in
     space/ then reads that state and places the agents at plane.ts positions.
  3. The USB image in QEMU, before any hardware. Unpack plan9-usb.img.bz2 (242
     MB compressed) and boot it as QEMU's disk, with the same VESA graphics
     set-up. If the floor draws there, the same image is the one to put on a USB
     stick for real hardware.

  Corrections to the forwarded text and the Supermicro note:
  - draw(2) images have no Pix field. The pixels live in the draw device; you
    get them back with unloadimage() or writeimage().
  - zlazy reads no files. It only answers whether a sentence belongs to a
    grammar. A "lazy template that fetches /usr/space/users/…" would be a new
    program.
  - 8th Edition Unix can't join. It predates the 9P protocol, so it can't join a
    Plan 9 namespace. Plan 9 under QEMU, on GCE, or on the Raspberry Pi can.
  - 9pserve isn't in 9legacy. It's a plan9port tool.
  - The 144 workers aren't Plan 9 processes. In boxedPINE they are browser
    worker scopes, so they can't be observed inside the guest. The eight agents
    can, because the floor runs them there.
  - The Supermicro boards are unconfirmed. I haven't verified 9legacy's support
    for them; check their network chips against 9legacy's driver list before
    buying.
  - The A-Frame "4D" framing is a metaphor. Position, rotation and scale exist
    the same way in three.js.

  omi.org and index.org (the curated workspace index for ~/devops) are read. The
  audit work, if it's indexed there, would go under its project surfaces.

● These files settle the "3.5D or 4D" question in the project's own terms,
  without the forwarded octave/fifth assignments. The vault's root note has a
  dimensional ladder, and the XR view lands on it directly:

  Ladder: 0D
  Component: observer (PannerNode position)
  In the XR view: the viewer's position in the XR session, which is also where
  sound is heard from
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 1D
  Component: DOMPoint
  In the XR view: each agent's place on the plane, plane.ts's (u, v, layer)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 2D
  Component: Media Track
  In the XR view: the Plan 9 guest's frame, carried as a video track with the
  narrative VTT cues on it
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 3D
  Component: DOMRect
  In the XR view: hit zones: picking an agent, as plane.ts's nearest does
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 4D
  Component: DOMMatrix, composition of overlays
  In the XR view: the viewer's pose (XRRigidTransform.matrix, the 16-number
  array
  src/README.v2.md cites) composed with each agent's transform; your "range and
   hit list overlays" compose here
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 6D
  Component: Canvas
  In the XR view: the rendering surface, an OffscreenCanvas
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 7D
  Component: Event Loop, cuechange-driven ticks
  In the XR view: the animation frame, which is the cue length you settled
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Ladder: 9D
  Component: Network Mesh
  In the XR view: others joining over 9P

  So XR is the 4D step: a composition of matrices over the 2D frame and the 1D
  places, seen from the 0D observer. Nothing needs to assign agents to the 12,
  16 or 20 layers. clock.md (now in _deprecated) is where the 12/16/20 numbers
  come from, but it doesn't assign agents to layers. The forwarded text added
  that part, and it isn't derived from anything.

  Stage 1 in those terms. The guest's VGA frames, taken through the monitor, are
  drawn to a canvas. captureStream() turns that canvas into a 2D media track: a
  video. The video becomes a texture in space/xr.html's scene, with a narrative
  VTT track attached so its cue changes drive the ticks (7D). The viewer's pose
  supplies the 4D. None of this needs changes to the guest, and it uses only
  what already runs.

❯ the layers were for like events and intent framing like seing inside the mind
  of an agent,  like a 4d interface from when character go 4d in movies and
  can speak to the audiene, its our interjection directly to agents instead of
  as an agent like the IS|OF of the GOD and WORD
  That reframes it. The 12/16/20 layers aren't spatial height — they're intent
  frames. That's the fourth wall, not the fourth dimension.

  What the layers actually are
  The layers are event frames in the sense a 4D film uses: a character who can
  address the audience directly is operating in a frame the ordinary camera
  can't see. The observer's 0D is the audience seat; the agent's 1D is its
  place on the plane; the layers above the plane are the agent's internal
  frames — the ones the agent reads when deciding what to do.

  Layer   What it holds   Where the reader sees it
  1D (place)      the agent's position (u, v)     the floor, where everyone can
  see it
  2D (media)      the agent's state over time     the video track, the cues
  3D (hit)        the agent's intent zones        what you can click and how it
  reacts
  4D (matrix)     the agent's frame of reference  the pose you see when you
  interject
  6D (canvas)     the agent's self-model  what it draws about itself
  7D (event)      the agent's clock       the ticks it feels
  9D (mesh)       the agent's peers       what it knows about others
  The IS|OF slot is exactly this: when you say "GOD is WORD", the agent is
  identifying with the frame. When you say "GOD of WORD", the agent is
  referring to the frame from outside. That's the difference between speaking
  as the audience (IS) and speaking to the audience (OF).

  What the XR interface does
  The XR view is where you interject. Not as an agent — as the observer who is
  outside every agent's frame but can address any of them.

  The mechanism is the one Observe.c already has:

  read: what the agent is at now (its state).

  write: an interjection (it takes effect).

  constrain: a request (it doesn't take effect unless the agent chooses).

  The XR view adds the fourth wall: you can see the agent's frame (the 4D
  matrix), and you can step into it (interject). The step is what makes it 4D
  rather than 3D — the observer moves inside the agent's frame, not through the
  space around it.

  The mapping, in code terms
  The A-Frame entity's rotation (or the three.js matrix) is the agent's frame.
  The agent's intent file is its intent (the IS/OF). The agent's state file is
  its present (the 2D media). And the observer's pose is the audience seat (the
  0D).

  So the XR scene has four things per agent:

  A-Frame File    What it is
  position        pos     the agent's place (1D)
  rotation        rot     the agent's frame (4D)
  src (texture)   frame   the agent's present (2D)
  data-intent     intent  the agent's IS/OF
  And the observer's pose is the audience seat. When the observer enters an
  agent's frame (walks into its rotation), the agent's intent is the IS/OF of
  the interjection.

  The one thing to confirm
  The IS/OF is the choice between two interjections:

  IS: "GOD is WORD" — the interjection identifies the agent with the frame.

  OF: "GOD of WORD" — the interjection refers to the agent from outside the
  frame.

  Which one is the observer's interjection? Or is the observer's interjection
  the choice between them (the IS|OF slot)?

  I'd say the second: the observer's interjection is the IS/OF slot. The
  observer chooses whether to speak as the agent (IS) or to the agent (OF).
  That's the fourth-wall interjection.

  Is that the right reading, or does the observer have a third mode (e.g.,
  silence, or a mode that isn't IS/OF)?

  yes its the 5th index and may be the 6th, i dont remember, but its the only
  indecies that show the difference
  The IS/OF slot is the fifth and sixth indices — the only ones that expose the
  difference. That's the key: the agent's frame is four indices (place, state,
  intent, present), and the difference between two agents' frames is the fifth
  (IS) and sixth (OF). Those are the only indices where the observer's
  interjection lands.

  What the six indices are
  Read the agent's frame as six positions:

  Index   What it is      Where
  1       the place (u, v)        1D
  2       the state (the 16-bit word)     2D
  3       the intent (the IS/OF slot)     the interjection point
  4       the present (the frame) 2D media
  5       the difference the observer sees        IS — the identification
  6       the difference the observer makes       OF — the reference
  The first four are the agent's own frame. The fifth and sixth are the
  observer's frame — the two ways the observer can enter.

  And the "difference" is literal: it's the XOR between what the agent is and
  what the observer expects. That XOR is the only thing the interjection
  carries. That's why the fifth and sixth are the only indices that show the
  difference.

  The A-Frame mapping
  The agent's A-Frame entity has:

  html
  <a-entity
    position="u v 0"          <!-- 1: place -->
    data-state="0x4D45"       <!-- 2: state -->
    data-intent="IS"          <!-- 3: intent -->
    material="src: #frame"    <!-- 4: present -->
    data-diff-is="0x0000"     <!-- 5: IS difference -->
    data-diff-of="0x0100"     <!-- 6: OF difference -->
  ></a-entity>
  And the observer's interjection is the choice of which difference to read:

  IS: the observer identifies with the agent — the difference is 0x0000 (they
  agree).

  OF: the observer refers to the agent — the difference is 0x0100 (they
  differ).

  The fifth index is where the observer becomes the agent (IS). The sixth is
  where the observer refers to the agent (OF). Both show the difference. The
  choice between them is the interjection.

  What this means for the XR scene
  The XR view shows, per agent:

  The agent's frame (indices 1–4), which is the A-Frame entity.

  The observer's difference (indices 5–6), which is the overlay on the entity.

  And the observer can switch between the two:

  Press IS → the entity's data-diff-is overlay appears (the agreement, 0x0000).

  Press OF → the entity's data-diff-of overlay appears (the disagreement,
  0x0100).

  So the XR scene has a toggle per agent: IS or OF. That's the observer's
  interjection. The fourth wall is the toggle.

  The 12/16/20 question, resolved
  The 12/16/20 were never the spatial layers. They're the difference bases:

  12 = the fifth (the IS reading) — the difference in the 12-tone lattice.

  16 = the octave (the OF reading) — the difference in the 16-control lattice.

  20 = the hypotenuse (the magnitude) — the difference in the 20-face lattice.

  So the IS overlay is 12-based, the OF overlay is 16-based, and the magnitude
  is 20-based. The observer's interjection is the choice between 12 and 16,
  scaled by 20.

  The one thing to confirm
  The six indices are the agent's frame. Is the observer's interjection:

  A) A single toggle per agent (IS or OF).

  B) A separate IS and OF overlay per agent (both visible at once).

  C) A per-agent choice that sets the agent's data-intent (the observer changes
  the agent's frame).

  A is the simplest: the observer presses one button and sees the IS or OF
  difference. B is richer: the observer sees both differences side by side. C
  is the strongest: the observer's choice changes the agent's frame (the
  interjection takes effect).

  The Observe.c model has all three: read (A), constrain (B), write (C). So the
  XR scene could offer all three, with the observer choosing which mode to be
  in.

  Which mode is the default for the XR scene: read (A), constrain (B), or write
  (C)?

● You remembered right: it's both the 5th and the 6th.

  - GOD IS WORD against GOD OF WORD differs only at the 5th and 6th characters:
    I/O XOR to 06, and S/F to 15.
  - WORD IS GOD against WORD OF GOD differs at the 6th and 7th instead, because
    WORD is one letter longer than GOD.
  - Counting from zero, those are indices 4–5 and 5–6. AGENTS.md says to keep
    human ordinals and zero-based indices separate, so the interface should
    label them as ordinals.
  - The difference itself, 06 15, is the same in both pairs. That's the only
    thing an interjection in the IS|OF slot carries.

  Corrections to the forwarded text. Its six-index table (place, state, intent,
  present, IS difference, OF difference) and its values 0x0000 and 0x0100 are
  invented. The real difference is 06 15, at the ordinals above. Its "12 is the
  IS reading, 16 the OF reading" is invented too: nothing in clock.md or the
  codex assigns them.

  Your framing in terms of what exists. "Interjecting directly to agents instead
  of as an agent" is the Observer's position. observe.c stands outside every
  agent and already has three ways in:
  - read (see the agent's state);
  - constrain (a request the agent's own orbit decides; nothing is written);
  - write (an interjection: stop, write, start).

  The IS|OF slot is where the fourth-wall interjection is spoken:
  - IS speaks as the frame (identity).
  - OF speaks of the frame, from outside (relation).

  They already have a mechanism: the cast's IS/OF sets FLAG, and FLAG turns the
  PL360 step.

  Recommended default for the XR view: read.
  - Constrain is safe as a second mode, because it never moves an agent.
  - Write is exactly where IS|OF lands. It can move an agent onto another orbit,
    as SOLOMON's accidental interjection showed, so it should take a deliberate
    step in the interface, not a hover.

  All of this needs stage 2: the agents' state served from Plan 9 so the room
  can show and address each one. That's an httpd(2) server in the guest, which
  reads through mach(2) like observe.c and writes only on an explicit
  interjection, plus a port forward in run.sh, which means restarting QEMU.

  Should I build stage 2 with read as the default?
❯ build stage 2 with read as the default

● Building stage 2: the agents' state served by Plan 9 itself, shown in the room
  with read as the default.

  In the guest: space/agentd.c. It handles one HTTP request per connection,
  started by Plan 9's own aux/listen1 on port 8000. It reads the running floor
  with mach(2), like observe.c:
  - GET /agents reads each agent's tag, hue, state and point, plus the tick, as
    JSON. This is the default.
  - GET /constrain?… makes a constraint request. It writes nothing.
  - POST /interject?… is the one write: stop, write, start. It only answers to
    POST, so reading can never write.

  On the host: one more port forward in run.sh, which means restarting QEMU and
  setting up the screen again.

  In the room: the eight agents as spheres on the floor at their plane.ts
  positions, coloured by hue and labelled with their live state. Read is the
  default mode; interjecting needs a confirm step.
